People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess what brings me in is, I don’t really want to do anything anymore. Not the things that usually make me feel like myself, you know?

I used to go to salsa nights — I mean, I wasn’t some professional dancer or anything, but I liked the music, the people, the whole feeling of being there. And church gatherings, too… I’d usually show up, talk with folks, maybe help set up or bring something. I used to cook for the neighbors sometimes, just because I enjoyed it. It felt good to feed people.

And my shop… I’m a carpenter, so that’s always been kind of my place. Sometimes I’d walk in there even when I didn’t have a job to do, just to smell the wood and look around, maybe mess with a piece of lumber. Lately I don’t even want to do that. I’ll stand there thinking I should go in, and then I just don’t. It’s like the wanting part is gone. That’s what scared me enough to come in.

No… not two years. It’s been long, but not that long. I’d say about six months now, maybe around there. Long enough that I’ve stopped telling myself it’s just a rough week or I’m tired from work or regular life stress.

At first I kept thinking, “Well, everybody has a slump,” you know? Especially at my age, with work and responsibilities and all that. But it just kept dragging on, day after day, and now it feels like it’s become this heavy stretch of time rather than something that passes. Yeah… yeah, most of the time I have. It’s not just a bad mood here and there. Most mornings I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling right in my chest, like before I even open my eyes all the way I already know the day is going to feel gray.

It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like the color has gone out of the house. Everything’s still there — my wife, the rooms, the tools, the same life I’ve had — but it all feels far away or drained somehow. And I find myself trying to keep it together first thing in the morning, because I don’t want to break down in front of my wife. I’ll be lying there or sitting on the edge of the bed, just forcing myself to breathe normal and act like I’m okay.

So yes… for most of these months, I’d say I’ve been feeling down. More than I want to admit.

the shop rubbing my hands and not actually doing anything useful.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: Even after sitting down or getting a full night in bed, my body feels worn out like I have been framing a roof all day, and small chores like loading tools into the truck feel like too much.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep replaying every appointment and every change in my wife’s health, telling myself a better husband would have caught it sooner or protected her somehow.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I stare at blueprints I have read my whole life and the lines blur together in my head, so I end up asking the younger guys to repeat things I should understand right away.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I have had thoughts like maybe it would be easier if I did not wake up, and sometimes I catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me, even though that scares me.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: At the beginning of all this I had stretches where I felt fired up and almost unstoppable, but if my wife or one of the crew questioned me I would snap at them over nothing.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: I was running on this surge where I could not sit still, sketching workshop ideas late into the night, calling suppliers, and looking for the next thing to fix before I had finished the first.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I honestly believed I could turn my little operation into the best boutique restoration shop around and handle my wife’s care and every job at the same time without dropping a thing.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: When I was in that keyed-up state, every saw noise, phone buzz, or bit of movement in the shop pulled me off track, and I would jump from one half-done task to another.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I took on extra restoration jobs, started mentoring the younger carpenters harder than usual, drew up business plans, and reached out to people I normally would have waited weeks to call.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I have had to hand off parts of jobs I normally supervise myself, and I have pulled away from church, friends, and neighbors to the point that my wife and parish contact got worried about my safety.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This whole change in my mood and behavior has been going on for about two months, since my wife’s health suddenly declined and our routine changed.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 6 months\n  manifestation: The depression has been dragging on for about six months now, long enough that it no longer feels like just a bad week or ordinary stress.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 55 / carpenter / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S025\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is, I don’t really want to do anything anymore. Not the things that usually make me feel like myself, you know?\n\nI used to go to salsa nights — I mean, I wasn’t some professional dancer or anything, but I liked the music, the people, the whole feeling of being there. And church gatherings, too… I’d usually show up, talk with folks, maybe help set up or bring something. I used to cook for the neighbors sometimes, just because I enjoyed it. It felt good to feed people.\n\nAnd my shop… I’m a carpenter, so that’s always been kind of my place. Sometimes I’d walk in there even when I didn’t have a job to do, just to smell the wood and look around, maybe mess with a piece of lumber. Lately I don’t even want to do that. I’ll stand there thinking I should go in, and then I just don’t. It’s like the wanting part is gone. That’s what scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Has this loss of interest been going on most days for two years or more?\n\nPatient: No… not two years. It’s been long, but not that long. I’d say about six months now, maybe around there. Long enough that I’ve stopped telling myself it’s just a rough week or I’m tired from work or regular life stress.\n\nAt first I kept thinking, “Well, everybody has a slump,” you know? Especially at my age, with work and responsibilities and all that. But it just kept dragging on, day after day, and now it feels like it’s become this heavy stretch of time rather than something that passes. Six months doesn’t sound as big as two years, I guess, but living inside it… it feels like a long time.\n\nDoctor: For most of these past six months, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, most of the time I have. It’s not just a bad mood here and there. Most mornings I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling right in my chest, like before I even open my eyes all the way I already know the day is going to feel gray.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like the color has gone out of the house. Everything’s still there — my wife, the rooms, the tools, the same life I’ve had — but it all feels far away or drained somehow. And I find myself trying to keep it together first thing in the morning, because I don’t want to break down in front of my wife. I’ll be lying there or sitting on the edge of the bed, just forcing myself to breathe normal and act like I’m okay.\n\nSo yes… for most of these months, I’d say I’ve been feeling down. More than I want to admit.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid this start after something stressful or upsetting happened in your life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it did. It’s hard to separate it all out, because life doesn’t stop and start cleanly, you know, but the real change seemed to begin after my wife’s health suddenly got worse.

That was about two months ago. Before that, I wasn’t perfect or anything, but we had our routine. We knew who did what in the mornings, what evenings looked like, how the house kind of ran. And then when her health declined, everything shifted pretty fast. The days started revolving around that — around what she needed, what had to change, what I had to keep track of — and I don’t think I ever really caught my breath after that.

So, yes… if I’m being honest, this whole change in my mood and how I’ve been acting really started around then, when her health took that turn and our normal routine kind of fell apart.

Especially at my age, with work and responsibilities and all that. But it just kept dragging on, day after day, and now it feels like it’s become this heavy stretch of time rather than something that passes. Six months doesn’t sound as big as two years, I guess, but living inside it… it feels like a long time.\n\nDoctor: For most of these past six months, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, most of the time I have. It’s not just a bad mood here and there. Most mornings I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling right in my chest, like before I even open my eyes all the way I already know the day is going to feel gray.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like the color has gone out of the house. Everything’s still there — my wife, the rooms, the tools, the same life I’ve had — but it all feels far away or drained somehow. And I find myself trying to keep it together first thing in the morning, because I don’t want to break down in front of my wife. I’ll be lying there or sitting on the edge of the bed, just forcing myself to breathe normal and act like I’m okay.\n\nSo yes… for most of these months, I’d say I’ve been feeling down. More than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful or upsetting happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did. It’s hard to separate it all out, because life doesn’t stop and start cleanly, you know, but the real change seemed to begin after my wife’s health suddenly got worse.\n\nThat was about two months ago. Before that, I wasn’t perfect or anything, but we had our routine. We knew who did what in the mornings, what evenings looked like, how the house kind of ran. And then when her health declined, everything shifted pretty fast. The days started revolving around that — around what she needed, what had to change, what I had to keep track of — and I don’t think I ever really caught my breath after that.\n\nSo, yes… if I’m being honest, this whole change in my mood and how I’ve been acting really started around then, when her health took that turn and our normal routine kind of fell apart.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore your wife’s health got worse, were you already feeling depressed most days?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I was. Maybe I didn’t call it depressed at the time, because I kept trying to explain it away as just being worn down or getting older or needing a weekend to catch up. But looking back, most mornings even before things got worse with my wife, I’d wake up with that same heavy, empty feeling in my chest.

It’s like I’d open my eyes and the day already felt flat before anything had even happened. The house would be the same house, you know — same rooms, same light coming in, same life around me — but it felt like the color had been drained out of it. I’d lie there or sit on the edge of the bed and have to kind of gather myself before I could go out and face her, because I didn’t want her seeing me fall apart first thing in the morning.

So… yes. I guess the honest answer is yes, I was already feeling that way most days. Her health getting worse made everything heavier, but that feeling was there before.

Yeah… I mean, I think so. I don’t know if I can put neat dates around it, but this isn’t the first time I’ve had that kind of stretch where most days I woke up feeling low.

It’s that same thing I was trying to describe — mornings are the worst. I’ll wake up and before I even really think about the day, there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting in my chest. Like the house is the same, my life is the same, but somehow the color’s gone out of it. Everything feels dimmer and farther away.

And I remember having times before these last six months where it was like that for more than just a few bad days. I’d be lying there or sitting on the edge of the bed, trying to get myself together, trying not to crack or start crying before my wife saw me. Just forcing myself to look normal, breathe normal, act like I could start the day.

So… yeah. I’d say I’ve had other periods like that before, where I felt depressed most days for at least a couple weeks.

I’m not sure I can say that cleanly. I’ve definitely had stretches before where I felt low for a while, but whether one of them lasted a full two months and then completely went away… I don’t know.

Part of the problem is I don’t think I was tracking it that way at the time. I’d just keep working, keep doing what needed doing, and then later I’d realize, “Oh, I guess I haven’t felt like myself in a long time.” But I couldn’t tell you exactly when it started or stopped.

And “fully went away” is hard for me to answer too. There have been times I felt better, sure — more like myself, more able to get through the day — but I don’t know if I’d say it completely lifted for a while in a clear way. I’d hate to give you a definite yes if I’m not really sure.

behavior has been going on for about two months, since my wife’s health suddenly declined and our routine changed.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 6 months\n  manifestation: The depression has been dragging on for about six months now, long enough that it no longer feels like just a bad week or ordinary stress.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 55 / carpenter / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S025\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Before your wife’s health got worse, were you already feeling depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I was. Maybe I didn’t call it depressed at the time, because I kept trying to explain it away as just being worn down or getting older or needing a weekend to catch up. But looking back, most mornings even before things got worse with my wife, I’d wake up with that same heavy, empty feeling in my chest.\n\nIt’s like I’d open my eyes and the day already felt flat before anything had even happened. The house would be the same house, you know — same rooms, same light coming in, same life around me — but it felt like the color had been drained out of it. I’d lie there or sit on the edge of the bed and have to kind of gather myself before I could go out and face her, because I didn’t want her seeing me fall apart first thing in the morning.\n\nSo… yes. Her health getting worse made everything heavier, but that feeling was there before.\n\nDoctor: Before these past six months, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. I don’t know if I can put neat dates around it, but this isn’t the first time I’ve had that kind of stretch where most days I woke up feeling low.\n\nIt’s that same thing I was trying to describe — mornings are the worst. I’ll wake up and before I even really think about the day, there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting in my chest. Like the house is the same, my life is the same, but somehow the color’s gone out of it. Everything feels dimmer and farther away.\n\nAnd I remember having times before these last six months where it was like that for more than just a few bad days. I’d be lying there or sitting on the edge of the bed, trying to get myself together, trying not to crack or start crying before my wife saw me. Just forcing myself to look normal, breathe normal, act like I could start the day.\n\nSo… yeah. I’d say I’ve had other periods like that before, where I felt depressed most days for at least a couple weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, did you ever have a separate episode of depression that lasted at least two months and then fully went away for a while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that cleanly. I’ve definitely had stretches before where I felt low for a while, but whether one of them lasted a full two months and then completely went away… I don’t know.\n\nPart of the problem is I don’t think I was tracking it that way at the time. I’d just keep working, keep doing what needed doing, and then later I’d realize, “Oh, I guess I haven’t felt like myself in a long time.” But I couldn’t tell you exactly when it started or stopped.\n\nAnd “fully went away” is hard for me to answer too. There have been times I felt better, sure — more like myself, more able to get through the day — but I don’t know if I’d say it completely lifted for a while in a clear way. I’d hate to give you a definite yes if I’m not really sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nBetween this depressed period and any earlier depressed period, was there ever a time when you felt completely back to your usual self?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I can honestly say that. I mean… there were times I was functioning better, sure. Times when I wasn’t waking up every single morning feeling like I had to drag myself up out of a hole. I could get through work, talk to people, maybe even seem pretty normal from the outside.

But “completely back to my usual self” is where I get stuck. I’m not sure I’d know exactly where to draw that line. Looking back, it feels more like things would ease up for a while, like the pressure backed off some, but I don’t know if I ever really sat there and thought, “Okay, I’m fully myself again.” I wasn’t keeping track of it that way.

So… I guess my best answer is I’m not sure. Maybe partially better at times, but I can’t say for certain there was a clear stretch where I felt completely back to normal between them.

I could get through work, talk to people, maybe even seem pretty normal from the outside.\n\nBut “completely back to my usual self” is where I get stuck. I’m not sure I’d know exactly where to draw that line. Looking back, it feels more like things would ease up for a while, like the pressure backed off some, but I don’t know if I ever really sat there and thought, “Okay, I’m fully myself again.” I wasn’t keeping track of it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my best answer is I’m not sure. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes. Before these last six months, there were times where it wasn’t just a bad day or two. It would go on, morning after morning, for at least a couple weeks.

The clearest way I can describe it is I’d wake up and immediately feel this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, before anything had even happened. Like I’d open my eyes and the house would be the same house, my wife would be there, the day would be starting like any other day, but it felt like all the color had been drained out of everything. And I’d just lie there thinking, “How am I supposed to get up and act normal?”

I remember having to really force myself not to fall apart in front of my wife. Like trying to hold my face together, get dressed, make coffee, do the regular things, while inside I felt like I was already breaking down. So… yeah, it definitely lasted at least two weeks in a row before.

Everything’s still there — my wife, the rooms, the tools, the same life I’ve had — but it all feels far away or drained somehow. And I find myself trying to keep it together first thing in the morning, because I don’t want to break down in front of my wife. Before that, I wasn’t perfect or anything, but we had our routine. We knew who did what in the mornings, what evenings looked like, how the house kind of ran. Maybe I didn’t call it depressed at the time, because I kept trying to explain it away as just being worn down or getting older or needing a weekend to catch up. The days started revolving around that — around what she needed, what had to change, what I had to keep track of — and I don’t think I ever really caught my breath after that.\n\nSo, yes… if I’m being honest, this whole change in my mood and how I’ve been acting really started around then, when her health took that turn and our normal routine kind of fell apart.\n\nDoctor: Before your wife’s health got worse, were you already feeling depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I was. Maybe I didn’t call it depressed at the time, because I kept trying to explain it away as just being worn down or getting older or needing a weekend to catch up. But looking back, most mornings even before things got worse with my wife, I’d wake up with that same heavy, empty feeling in my chest.\n\nIt’s like I’d open my eyes and the day already felt flat before anything had even happened. The house would be the same house, you know — same rooms, same light coming in, same life around me — but it felt like the color had been drained out of it. I’d lie there or sit on the edge of the bed and have to kind of gather myself before I could go out and face her, because I didn’t want her seeing me fall apart first thing in the morning.\n\nSo… yes. I guess the honest answer is yes, I was already feeling that way most days. Her health getting worse made everything heavier, but that feeling was there before.\n\nDoctor: Before these past six months, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. I don’t know if I can put neat dates around it, but this isn’t the first time I’ve had that kind of stretch where most days I woke up feeling low.\n\nIt’s that same thing I was trying to describe — mornings are the worst. I’ll wake up and before I even really think about the day, there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting in my chest. Like the house is the same, my life is the same, but somehow the color’s gone out of it. Everything feels dimmer and farther away.\n\nAnd I remember having times before these last six months where it was like that for more than just a few bad days. I’d be lying there or sitting on the edge of the bed, trying to get myself together, trying not to crack or start crying before my wife saw me. Just forcing myself to look normal, breathe normal, act like I could start the day.\n\nSo… yeah. I’d say I’ve had other periods like that before, where I felt depressed most days for at least a couple weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, did you ever have a separate episode of depression that lasted at least two months and then fully went away for a while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that cleanly. I’ve definitely had stretches before where I felt low for a while, but whether one of them lasted a full two months and then completely went away… I don’t know.\n\nPart of the problem is I don’t think I was tracking it that way at the time. I’d just keep working, keep doing what needed doing, and then later I’d realize, “Oh, I guess I haven’t felt like myself in a long time.” But I couldn’t tell you exactly when it started or stopped.\n\nAnd “fully went away” is hard for me to answer too. There have been times I felt better, sure — more like myself, more able to get through the day — but I don’t know if I’d say it completely lifted for a while in a clear way. I’d hate to give you a definite yes if I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Between this depressed period and any earlier depressed period, was there ever a time when you felt completely back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can honestly say that. I mean… there were times I was functioning better, sure. Times when I wasn’t waking up every single morning feeling like I had to drag myself up out of a hole. I could get through work, talk to people, maybe even seem pretty normal from the outside.\n\nBut “completely back to my usual self” is where I get stuck. I’m not sure I’d know exactly where to draw that line. Looking back, it feels more like things would ease up for a while, like the pressure backed off some, but I don’t know if I ever really sat there and thought, “Okay, I’m fully myself again.” I wasn’t keeping track of it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my best answer is I’m not sure. Maybe partially better at times, but I can’t say for certain there was a clear stretch where I felt completely back to normal between them.\n\nDoctor: Before these past six months, was there ever a time when feeling depressed lasted at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. Before these last six months, there were times where it wasn’t just a bad day or two. It would go on, morning after morning, for at least a couple weeks.\n\nThe clearest way I can describe it is I’d wake up and immediately feel this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, before anything had even happened. Like I’d open my eyes and the house would be the same house, my wife would be there, the day would be starting like any other day, but it felt like all the color had been drained out of everything. And I’d just lie there thinking, “How am I supposed to get up and act normal?”\n\nI remember having to really force myself not to fall apart in front of my wife. Like trying to hold my face together, get dressed, make coffee, do the regular things, while inside I felt like I was already breaking down. So… yeah, it definitely lasted at least two weeks in a row before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve been upset and tense at times, sure, but if you’re asking whether anxiety or worry has been a big, constant thing alongside the depression… I’m not sure I’ve thought of it that way.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m honestly not sure. Two months is a pretty specific stretch, and I don’t know that I was ever tracking it closely enough to say, “Yes, that was definitely two full months where I was completely back to my usual self.”

There were periods where I was better than the worst of it, definitely. Like I could get up, go to work, get through the day, talk to people without feeling like I was barely holding myself together every second. But whether that means I was really back to myself… I don’t know. Sometimes when you’re in it for long enough, “better” starts to feel like normal just because it’s not as bad as the bad times.

So I guess the most honest answer is I can’t say for sure. I don’t remember a clear two-month period where I felt fully back to normal, but I also wasn’t marking it on a calendar or anything.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.9, F32.9, F43.2, F41.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is, I don’t really want to do anything anymore. Not the things that usually make me feel like myself, you know?\n\nI used to go to salsa nights — I mean, I wasn’t some professional dancer or anything, but I liked the music, the people, the whole feeling of being there. And church gatherings, too… I’d usually show up, talk with folks, maybe help set up or bring something. I used to cook for the neighbors sometimes, just because I enjoyed it. It felt good to feed people.\n\nAnd my shop… I’m a carpenter, so that’s always been kind of my place. Sometimes I’d walk in there even when I didn’t have a job to do, just to smell the wood and look around, maybe mess with a piece of lumber. Lately I don’t even want to do that. I’ll stand there thinking I should go in, and then I just don’t. It’s like the wanting part is gone. That’s what scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Has this loss of interest been going on most days for two years or more?\n\nPatient: No… not two years. It’s been long, but not that long. I’d say about six months now, maybe around there. Long enough that I’ve stopped telling myself it’s just a rough week or I’m tired from work or regular life stress.\n\nAt first I kept thinking, “Well, everybody has a slump,” you know? Especially at my age, with work and responsibilities and all that. But it just kept dragging on, day after day, and now it feels like it’s become this heavy stretch of time rather than something that passes. Six months doesn’t sound as big as two years, I guess, but living inside it… it feels like a long time.\n\nDoctor: For most of these past six months, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, most of the time I have. It’s not just a bad mood here and there. Most mornings I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling right in my chest, like before I even open my eyes all the way I already know the day is going to feel gray.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like the color has gone out of the house. Everything’s still there — my wife, the rooms, the tools, the same life I’ve had — but it all feels far away or drained somehow. And I find myself trying to keep it together first thing in the morning, because I don’t want to break down in front of my wife. I’ll be lying there or sitting on the edge of the bed, just forcing myself to breathe normal and act like I’m okay.\n\nSo yes… for most of these months, I’d say I’ve been feeling down. More than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful or upsetting happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did. It’s hard to separate it all out, because life doesn’t stop and start cleanly, you know, but the real change seemed to begin after my wife’s health suddenly got worse.\n\nThat was about two months ago. Before that, I wasn’t perfect or anything, but we had our routine. We knew who did what in the mornings, what evenings looked like, how the house kind of ran. And then when her health declined, everything shifted pretty fast. The days started revolving around that — around what she needed, what had to change, what I had to keep track of — and I don’t think I ever really caught my breath after that.\n\nSo, yes… if I’m being honest, this whole change in my mood and how I’ve been acting really started around then, when her health took that turn and our normal routine kind of fell apart.\n\nDoctor: Before your wife’s health got worse, were you already feeling depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I was. Maybe I didn’t call it depressed at the time, because I kept trying to explain it away as just being worn down or getting older or needing a weekend to catch up. But looking back, most mornings even before things got worse with my wife, I’d wake up with that same heavy, empty feeling in my chest.\n\nIt’s like I’d open my eyes and the day already felt flat before anything had even happened. The house would be the same house, you know — same rooms, same light coming in, same life around me — but it felt like the color had been drained out of it. I’d lie there or sit on the edge of the bed and have to kind of gather myself before I could go out and face her, because I didn’t want her seeing me fall apart first thing in the morning.\n\nSo… yes. I guess the honest answer is yes, I was already feeling that way most days. Her health getting worse made everything heavier, but that feeling was there before.\n\nDoctor: Before these past six months, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. I don’t know if I can put neat dates around it, but this isn’t the first time I’ve had that kind of stretch where most days I woke up feeling low.\n\nIt’s that same thing I was trying to describe — mornings are the worst. I’ll wake up and before I even really think about the day, there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting in my chest. Like the house is the same, my life is the same, but somehow the color’s gone out of it. Everything feels dimmer and farther away.\n\nAnd I remember having times before these last six months where it was like that for more than just a few bad days. I’d be lying there or sitting on the edge of the bed, trying to get myself together, trying not to crack or start crying before my wife saw me. Just forcing myself to look normal, breathe normal, act like I could start the day.\n\nSo… yeah. I’d say I’ve had other periods like that before, where I felt depressed most days for at least a couple weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, did you ever have a separate episode of depression that lasted at least two months and then fully went away for a while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that cleanly. I’ve definitely had stretches before where I felt low for a while, but whether one of them lasted a full two months and then completely went away… I don’t know.\n\nPart of the problem is I don’t think I was tracking it that way at the time. I’d just keep working, keep doing what needed doing, and then later I’d realize, “Oh, I guess I haven’t felt like myself in a long time.” But I couldn’t tell you exactly when it started or stopped.\n\nAnd “fully went away” is hard for me to answer too. There have been times I felt better, sure — more like myself, more able to get through the day — but I don’t know if I’d say it completely lifted for a while in a clear way. I’d hate to give you a definite yes if I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Between this depressed period and any earlier depressed period, was there ever a time when you felt completely back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can honestly say that. I mean… there were times I was functioning better, sure. Times when I wasn’t waking up every single morning feeling like I had to drag myself up out of a hole. I could get through work, talk to people, maybe even seem pretty normal from the outside.\n\nBut “completely back to my usual self” is where I get stuck. I’m not sure I’d know exactly where to draw that line. Looking back, it feels more like things would ease up for a while, like the pressure backed off some, but I don’t know if I ever really sat there and thought, “Okay, I’m fully myself again.” I wasn’t keeping track of it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my best answer is I’m not sure. Maybe partially better at times, but I can’t say for certain there was a clear stretch where I felt completely back to normal between them.\n\nDoctor: Before these past six months, was there ever a time when feeling depressed lasted at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. Before these last six months, there were times where it wasn’t just a bad day or two. It would go on, morning after morning, for at least a couple weeks.\n\nThe clearest way I can describe it is I’d wake up and immediately feel this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, before anything had even happened. Like I’d open my eyes and the house would be the same house, my wife would be there, the day would be starting like any other day, but it felt like all the color had been drained out of everything. And I’d just lie there thinking, “How am I supposed to get up and act normal?”\n\nI remember having to really force myself not to fall apart in front of my wife. Like trying to hold my face together, get dressed, make coffee, do the regular things, while inside I felt like I was already breaking down. So… yeah, it definitely lasted at least two weeks in a row before.\n\nDoctor: Along with feeling depressed, have you been feeling anxious or worried much of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve been upset and tense at times, sure, but if you’re asking whether anxiety or worry has been a big, constant thing alongside the depression… I’m not sure I’ve thought of it that way.\n\nA lot of the time it feels less like I’m worrying about specific things and more like I’m just weighed down, if that makes sense. Like my mind isn’t necessarily racing through a list of fears, it’s more that everything feels hard to face. But then again, maybe that counts as anxiety? I don’t know.\n\nI wouldn’t want to tell you “yes, much of the time” if that means something specific, because I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as worry exactly. It’s more… heaviness, dread maybe, but not always worry in the usual sense.\n\nDoctor: At any time before this current six-month period, did you have a stretch of at least two months when you felt completely back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. Two months is a pretty specific stretch, and I don’t know that I was ever tracking it closely enough to say, “Yes, that was definitely two full months where I was completely back to my usual self.”\n\nThere were periods where I was better than the worst of it, definitely. Like I could get up, go to work, get through the day, talk to people without feeling like I was barely holding myself together every second. But whether that means I was really back to myself… I don’t know. Sometimes when you’re in it for long enough, “better” starts to feel like normal just because it’s not as bad as the bad times.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is I can’t say for sure. I don’t remember a clear two-month period where I felt fully back to normal, but I also wasn’t marking it on a calendar or anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more… heaviness, dread maybe, but not always worry in the usual sense.\n\nDoctor: At any time before this current six-month period, did you have a stretch of at least two months when you felt completely back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. Two months is a pretty specific stretch, and I don’t know that I was ever tracking it closely enough to say, “Yes, that was definitely two full months where I was completely back to my usual self.”\n\nThere were periods where I was better than the worst of it, definitely. Like I could get up, go to work, get through the day, talk to people without feeling like I was barely holding myself together every second. But whether that means I was really back to myself… I don’t know. Sometimes when you’re in it for long enough, “better” starts to feel like normal just because it’s not as bad as the bad times.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is I can’t say for sure. I don’t remember a clear two-month period where I felt fully back to normal, but I also wasn’t marking it on a calendar or anything.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore this current six-month depression, do you remember a past time when your depression completely went away for at least two months?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know you’re asking it a little differently, but… I still don’t think I can say that for sure. “Completely went away” is the part that trips me up, because there were definitely times where I wasn’t as low, where I could function more like myself and not feel like I was dragging myself through every hour. But if I’m being honest, I don’t remember being able to look back and say, yes, there were two solid months where it was just gone.

It’s kind of blurry in my memory. When things eased up, I’d just be relieved that I wasn’t at the bottom anymore. I wasn’t sitting there counting weeks or thinking, “Okay, this is remission,” or anything like that. I’d just get through work, come home, do what needed doing, and if I had a few decent weeks I’d almost be afraid to trust it.

So… I don’t know. I don’t have a clear memory of a full two months where the depression completely went away before this. I can say there were better stretches, but “completely gone for at least two months,” I’m not sure.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.9, F32.9, F43.2, F41.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is, I don’t really want to do anything anymore. Not the things that usually make me feel like myself, you know?\n\nI used to go to salsa nights — I mean, I wasn’t some professional dancer or anything, but I liked the music, the people, the whole feeling of being there. And church gatherings, too… I’d usually show up, talk with folks, maybe help set up or bring something. I used to cook for the neighbors sometimes, just because I enjoyed it. It felt good to feed people.\n\nAnd my shop… I’m a carpenter, so that’s always been kind of my place. Sometimes I’d walk in there even when I didn’t have a job to do, just to smell the wood and look around, maybe mess with a piece of lumber. Lately I don’t even want to do that. I’ll stand there thinking I should go in, and then I just don’t. It’s like the wanting part is gone. That’s what scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Has this loss of interest been going on most days for two years or more?\n\nPatient: No… not two years. It’s been long, but not that long. I’d say about six months now, maybe around there. Long enough that I’ve stopped telling myself it’s just a rough week or I’m tired from work or regular life stress.\n\nAt first I kept thinking, “Well, everybody has a slump,” you know? Especially at my age, with work and responsibilities and all that. But it just kept dragging on, day after day, and now it feels like it’s become this heavy stretch of time rather than something that passes. Six months doesn’t sound as big as two years, I guess, but living inside it… it feels like a long time.\n\nDoctor: For most of these past six months, have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, most of the time I have. It’s not just a bad mood here and there. Most mornings I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling right in my chest, like before I even open my eyes all the way I already know the day is going to feel gray.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s like the color has gone out of the house. Everything’s still there — my wife, the rooms, the tools, the same life I’ve had — but it all feels far away or drained somehow. And I find myself trying to keep it together first thing in the morning, because I don’t want to break down in front of my wife. I’ll be lying there or sitting on the edge of the bed, just forcing myself to breathe normal and act like I’m okay.\n\nSo yes… for most of these months, I’d say I’ve been feeling down. More than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful or upsetting happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did. It’s hard to separate it all out, because life doesn’t stop and start cleanly, you know, but the real change seemed to begin after my wife’s health suddenly got worse.\n\nThat was about two months ago. Before that, I wasn’t perfect or anything, but we had our routine. We knew who did what in the mornings, what evenings looked like, how the house kind of ran. And then when her health declined, everything shifted pretty fast. The days started revolving around that — around what she needed, what had to change, what I had to keep track of — and I don’t think I ever really caught my breath after that.\n\nSo, yes… if I’m being honest, this whole change in my mood and how I’ve been acting really started around then, when her health took that turn and our normal routine kind of fell apart.\n\nDoctor: Before your wife’s health got worse, were you already feeling depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I was. Maybe I didn’t call it depressed at the time, because I kept trying to explain it away as just being worn down or getting older or needing a weekend to catch up. But looking back, most mornings even before things got worse with my wife, I’d wake up with that same heavy, empty feeling in my chest.\n\nIt’s like I’d open my eyes and the day already felt flat before anything had even happened. The house would be the same house, you know — same rooms, same light coming in, same life around me — but it felt like the color had been drained out of it. I’d lie there or sit on the edge of the bed and have to kind of gather myself before I could go out and face her, because I didn’t want her seeing me fall apart first thing in the morning.\n\nSo… yes. I guess the honest answer is yes, I was already feeling that way most days. Her health getting worse made everything heavier, but that feeling was there before.\n\nDoctor: Before these past six months, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. I don’t know if I can put neat dates around it, but this isn’t the first time I’ve had that kind of stretch where most days I woke up feeling low.\n\nIt’s that same thing I was trying to describe — mornings are the worst. I’ll wake up and before I even really think about the day, there’s this heavy, empty feeling sitting in my chest. Like the house is the same, my life is the same, but somehow the color’s gone out of it. Everything feels dimmer and farther away.\n\nAnd I remember having times before these last six months where it was like that for more than just a few bad days. I’d be lying there or sitting on the edge of the bed, trying to get myself together, trying not to crack or start crying before my wife saw me. Just forcing myself to look normal, breathe normal, act like I could start the day.\n\nSo… yeah. I’d say I’ve had other periods like that before, where I felt depressed most days for at least a couple weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month period, did you ever have a separate episode of depression that lasted at least two months and then fully went away for a while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that cleanly. I’ve definitely had stretches before where I felt low for a while, but whether one of them lasted a full two months and then completely went away… I don’t know.\n\nPart of the problem is I don’t think I was tracking it that way at the time. I’d just keep working, keep doing what needed doing, and then later I’d realize, “Oh, I guess I haven’t felt like myself in a long time.” But I couldn’t tell you exactly when it started or stopped.\n\nAnd “fully went away” is hard for me to answer too. There have been times I felt better, sure — more like myself, more able to get through the day — but I don’t know if I’d say it completely lifted for a while in a clear way. I’d hate to give you a definite yes if I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Between this depressed period and any earlier depressed period, was there ever a time when you felt completely back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can honestly say that. I mean… there were times I was functioning better, sure. Times when I wasn’t waking up every single morning feeling like I had to drag myself up out of a hole. I could get through work, talk to people, maybe even seem pretty normal from the outside.\n\nBut “completely back to my usual self” is where I get stuck. I’m not sure I’d know exactly where to draw that line. Looking back, it feels more like things would ease up for a while, like the pressure backed off some, but I don’t know if I ever really sat there and thought, “Okay, I’m fully myself again.” I wasn’t keeping track of it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my best answer is I’m not sure. Maybe partially better at times, but I can’t say for certain there was a clear stretch where I felt completely back to normal between them.\n\nDoctor: Before these past six months, was there ever a time when feeling depressed lasted at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. Before these last six months, there were times where it wasn’t just a bad day or two. It would go on, morning after morning, for at least a couple weeks.\n\nThe clearest way I can describe it is I’d wake up and immediately feel this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, before anything had even happened. Like I’d open my eyes and the house would be the same house, my wife would be there, the day would be starting like any other day, but it felt like all the color had been drained out of everything. And I’d just lie there thinking, “How am I supposed to get up and act normal?”\n\nI remember having to really force myself not to fall apart in front of my wife. Like trying to hold my face together, get dressed, make coffee, do the regular things, while inside I felt like I was already breaking down. So… yeah, it definitely lasted at least two weeks in a row before.\n\nDoctor: Along with feeling depressed, have you been feeling anxious or worried much of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve been upset and tense at times, sure, but if you’re asking whether anxiety or worry has been a big, constant thing alongside the depression… I’m not sure I’ve thought of it that way.\n\nA lot of the time it feels less like I’m worrying about specific things and more like I’m just weighed down, if that makes sense. Like my mind isn’t necessarily racing through a list of fears, it’s more that everything feels hard to face. But then again, maybe that counts as anxiety? I don’t know.\n\nI wouldn’t want to tell you “yes, much of the time” if that means something specific, because I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as worry exactly. It’s more… heaviness, dread maybe, but not always worry in the usual sense.\n\nDoctor: At any time before this current six-month period, did you have a stretch of at least two months when you felt completely back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. Looking back, it feels more like things would ease up for a while, like the pressure backed off some, but I don’t know if I ever really sat there and thought, “Okay, I’m fully myself again.” I wasn’t keeping track of it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my best answer is I’m not sure. Maybe partially better at times, but I can’t say for certain there was a clear stretch where I felt completely back to normal between them.\n\nDoctor: Before these past six months, was there ever a time when feeling depressed lasted at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. Before these last six months, there were times where it wasn’t just a bad day or two. It would go on, morning after morning, for at least a couple weeks.\n\nThe clearest way I can describe it is I’d wake up and immediately feel this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, before anything had even happened. Like I’d open my eyes and the house would be the same house, my wife would be there, the day would be starting like any other day, but it felt like all the color had been drained out of everything. And I’d just lie there thinking, “How am I supposed to get up and act normal?”\n\nI remember having to really force myself not to fall apart in front of my wife. Like trying to hold my face together, get dressed, make coffee, do the regular things, while inside I felt like I was already breaking down. So… yeah, it definitely lasted at least two weeks in a row before.\n\nDoctor: Along with feeling depressed, have you been feeling anxious or worried much of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve been upset and tense at times, sure, but if you’re asking whether anxiety or worry has been a big, constant thing alongside the depression… I’m not sure I’ve thought of it that way.\n\nA lot of the time it feels less like I’m worrying about specific things and more like I’m just weighed down, if that makes sense. Like my mind isn’t necessarily racing through a list of fears, it’s more that everything feels hard to face. But then again, maybe that counts as anxiety? I don’t know.\n\nI wouldn’t want to tell you “yes, much of the time” if that means something specific, because I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as worry exactly. It’s more… heaviness, dread maybe, but not always worry in the usual sense.\n\nDoctor: At any time before this current six-month period, did you have a stretch of at least two months when you felt completely back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. Two months is a pretty specific stretch, and I don’t know that I was ever tracking it closely enough to say, “Yes, that was definitely two full months where I was completely back to my usual self.”\n\nThere were periods where I was better than the worst of it, definitely. Like I could get up, go to work, get through the day, talk to people without feeling like I was barely holding myself together every second. But whether that means I was really back to myself… I don’t know. Sometimes when you’re in it for long enough, “better” starts to feel like normal just because it’s not as bad as the bad times.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is I can’t say for sure. I don’t remember a clear two-month period where I felt fully back to normal, but I also wasn’t marking it on a calendar or anything.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month depression, do you remember a past time when your depression completely went away for at least two months?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it a little differently, but… I still don’t think I can say that for sure. “Completely went away” is the part that trips me up, because there were definitely times where I wasn’t as low, where I could function more like myself and not feel like I was dragging myself through every hour. When things eased up, I’d just be relieved that I wasn’t at the bottom anymore. I wasn’t sitting there counting weeks or thinking, “Okay, this is remission,” or anything like that. I’d just get through work, come home, do what needed doing, and if I had a few decent weeks I’d almost be afraid to trust it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wish I could give you a clearer yes or no, but I really can’t say that I remember that happening in a clean way. I know you’re asking whether there was a solid stretch before these six months where it fully lifted and I felt like myself again for at least two months, and… I don’t have that in my memory as a definite thing.

There were times that were better. I don’t want to make it sound like it was always exactly the same level of bad. Sometimes I’d be functioning more, getting through my days, maybe not feeling quite so buried under it. But “completely lifted” and “back to normal” feels like a stronger statement than I’m comfortable making. I don’t remember thinking, “Okay, I’m myself again and this has been gone for a couple months.”

Part of the problem is I wasn’t measuring it that way. I’d just notice I was having less awful days, or I wasn’t struggling as visibly, and I’d sort of keep moving. But looking back now, it all kind of blends together. So I guess the honest answer is: I’m not sure. I don’t remember a clear two-month period where it completely went away and I felt fully normal.

I could get through work, talk to people, maybe even seem pretty normal from the outside.\n\nBut “completely back to my usual self” is where I get stuck. I’m not sure I’d know exactly where to draw that line. Looking back, it feels more like things would ease up for a while, like the pressure backed off some, but I don’t know if I ever really sat there and thought, “Okay, I’m fully myself again.” I wasn’t keeping track of it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my best answer is I’m not sure. Maybe partially better at times, but I can’t say for certain there was a clear stretch where I felt completely back to normal between them.\n\nDoctor: Before these past six months, was there ever a time when feeling depressed lasted at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. Before these last six months, there were times where it wasn’t just a bad day or two. It would go on, morning after morning, for at least a couple weeks.\n\nThe clearest way I can describe it is I’d wake up and immediately feel this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, before anything had even happened. Like I’d open my eyes and the house would be the same house, my wife would be there, the day would be starting like any other day, but it felt like all the color had been drained out of everything. And I’d just lie there thinking, “How am I supposed to get up and act normal?”\n\nI remember having to really force myself not to fall apart in front of my wife. Like trying to hold my face together, get dressed, make coffee, do the regular things, while inside I felt like I was already breaking down. So… yeah, it definitely lasted at least two weeks in a row before.\n\nDoctor: Along with feeling depressed, have you been feeling anxious or worried much of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve been upset and tense at times, sure, but if you’re asking whether anxiety or worry has been a big, constant thing alongside the depression… I’m not sure I’ve thought of it that way.\n\nA lot of the time it feels less like I’m worrying about specific things and more like I’m just weighed down, if that makes sense. Like my mind isn’t necessarily racing through a list of fears, it’s more that everything feels hard to face. But then again, maybe that counts as anxiety? I don’t know.\n\nI wouldn’t want to tell you “yes, much of the time” if that means something specific, because I’m not sure I’ve noticed it as worry exactly. It’s more… heaviness, dread maybe, but not always worry in the usual sense.\n\nDoctor: At any time before this current six-month period, did you have a stretch of at least two months when you felt completely back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. Two months is a pretty specific stretch, and I don’t know that I was ever tracking it closely enough to say, “Yes, that was definitely two full months where I was completely back to my usual self.”\n\nThere were periods where I was better than the worst of it, definitely. Like I could get up, go to work, get through the day, talk to people without feeling like I was barely holding myself together every second. But whether that means I was really back to myself… I don’t know. Sometimes when you’re in it for long enough, “better” starts to feel like normal just because it’s not as bad as the bad times.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is I can’t say for sure. I don’t remember a clear two-month period where I felt fully back to normal, but I also wasn’t marking it on a calendar or anything.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month depression, do you remember a past time when your depression completely went away for at least two months?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it a little differently, but… I still don’t think I can say that for sure. “Completely went away” is the part that trips me up, because there were definitely times where I wasn’t as low, where I could function more like myself and not feel like I was dragging myself through every hour. But if I’m being honest, I don’t remember being able to look back and say, yes, there were two solid months where it was just gone.\n\nIt’s kind of blurry in my memory. When things eased up, I’d just be relieved that I wasn’t at the bottom anymore. I wasn’t sitting there counting weeks or thinking, “Okay, this is remission,” or anything like that. I’d just get through work, come home, do what needed doing, and if I had a few decent weeks I’d almost be afraid to trust it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I don’t have a clear memory of a full two months where the depression completely went away before this. I can say there were better stretches, but “completely gone for at least two months,” I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before these past six months, was there ever a time when your depression completely lifted and you felt back to normal for at least two months?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clearer yes or no, but I really can’t say that I remember that happening in a clean way.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Bipolar II Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: At any time before this current six-month period, did you have a stretch of at least two months when you felt completely back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. Two months is a pretty specific stretch, and I don’t know that I was ever tracking it closely enough to say, “Yes, that was definitely two full months where I was completely back to my usual self.”\n\nThere were periods where I was better than the worst of it, definitely. Like I could get up, go to work, get through the day, talk to people without feeling like I was barely holding myself together every second. But whether that means I was really back to myself… I don’t know. Sometimes when you’re in it for long enough, “better” starts to feel like normal just because it’s not as bad as the bad times.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is I can’t say for sure. I don’t remember a clear two-month period where I felt fully back to normal, but I also wasn’t marking it on a calendar or anything.\n\nDoctor: Before this current six-month depression, do you remember a past time when your depression completely went away for at least two months?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it a little differently, but… I still don’t think I can say that for sure. “Completely went away” is the part that trips me up, because there were definitely times where I wasn’t as low, where I could function more like myself and not feel like I was dragging myself through every hour. But if I’m being honest, I don’t remember being able to look back and say, yes, there were two solid months where it was just gone.\n\nIt’s kind of blurry in my memory. When things eased up, I’d just be relieved that I wasn’t at the bottom anymore. I wasn’t sitting there counting weeks or thinking, “Okay, this is remission,” or anything like that. I’d just get through work, come home, do what needed doing, and if I had a few decent weeks I’d almost be afraid to trust it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I don’t have a clear memory of a full two months where the depression completely went away before this. I can say there were better stretches, but “completely gone for at least two months,” I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Before these past six months, was there ever a time when your depression completely lifted and you felt back to normal for at least two months?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clearer yes or no, but I really can’t say that I remember that happening in a clean way. I know you’re asking whether there was a solid stretch before these six months where it fully lifted and I felt like myself again for at least two months, and… I don’t have that in my memory as a definite thing.\n\nThere were times that were better. I don’t want to make it sound like it was always exactly the same level of bad. Sometimes I’d be functioning more, getting through my days, maybe not feeling quite so buried under it. But “completely lifted” and “back to normal” feels like a stronger statement than I’m comfortable making. I don’t remember thinking, “Okay, I’m myself again and this has been gone for a couple months.”\n\nPart of the problem is I wasn’t measuring it that way. I’d just notice I was having less awful days, or I wasn’t struggling as visibly, and I’d sort of keep moving. But looking back now, it all kind of blends together. So I guess the honest answer is: I’m not sure. I don’t remember a clear two-month period where it completely went away and I felt fully normal.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore this current six-month depression, was there ever a time when you felt completely back to your usual self for at least two months?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 55 / carpenter / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know I’ve kind of circled around this already, but no, I don’t have a clear memory of that. I mean, not in the way you’re asking — “completely back to my usual self” for two full months. That feels too definite for me to honestly say yes to.

There were stretches where I was more functional, where I could show up and do what I had to do and maybe not feel like everything was quite so heavy all the time. But looking back, I don’t know if that was really me being back to normal, or just me comparing it to the worse periods and thinking, well, at least this is better.

So I guess my answer is still that I’m not sure. I can’t point to a two-month stretch before this where I’d say I was fully myself again.
